It’s that for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nAnd management keeps saying, “No, look, here are the logs, it’s just policy,” but that’s exactly what bothers me. The logs are the thing that make it look official. So if you’re asking whether I’ve always gone around feeling targeted, no, I wouldn’t say that. This feels specific to what’s been happening at work, and it started with those process changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're being targeted or treated unfairly in any other areas of your life besides work?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn’t say I feel targeted everywhere or by random people or anything like that. It’s not like I think the grocery store clerk is watching me or my neighbors are involved. It’s very specifically work.\n\nThat’s part of why it bothers me so much, honestly, because I know how it sounds when I keep coming back to it, but it isn’t some general feeling that the world is against me. It’s tied to this one situation — the ticket rotation, the security audits, the way the documentation keeps lining up so that I’m the one who looks responsible after the fact. And then management keeps saying, “Here are the logs, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t actually reassure me, because the logs are the thing I’m worried they’re using to build the paper trail.\n\nSo outside of work, no, I don’t really have that same feeling. My concern is that at work, for over a year now, the system has been used in a way that makes it look official and neutral, when to me the pattern looks like they’re building a case against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past that might be contributing to your feelings of being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve thought about that, because I know people ask that kind of question for a reason, and I’m not trying to dismiss it. But I can’t point to some traumatic thing in my past that would explain this.\n\nI’ve had stressful periods, sure — work stress, family stress, the kind of things most people have by the time they’re my age — but nothing where I’d say, “Yes, that’s why I feel this way at work now.” This feels tied to what has actually been happening there, with the ticket rotation and the audits and the documentation. It doesn’t feel like an old wound getting stirred up, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, not that I’m aware of. I don’t have some past event that I think is driving it. My concern is really about the pattern at work now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been bullied or harassed at any point in your life, either in a workplace or elsewhere?\n\nPatient: Not in any major way, no. I mean, I’m sure there were times growing up where someone was unkind, or jobs where people were difficult, but I wouldn’t call it bullying or harassment in the way people usually mean that.\n\nI’ve had coworkers I didn’t get along with, managers who were impatient or dismissive, things like that. But nothing where I felt like I was being systematically bullied for years or anything outside of normal workplace conflict. I don’t have some long history of being singled out, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nThis situation at work now feels different to me, but in terms of earlier in my life, no — nothing really stands out as bullying or harassment that I’d point to as a big factor.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a situation in the past where you felt misled or deceived by someone in a position of authority?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of a clear example of that, no. I mean, I’ve had bosses or supervisors make decisions I didn’t agree with, or say one thing and then later the policy seemed to change, but I don’t know that I’d call that being deceived exactly.\n\nWhen you say “position of authority,” I’m thinking teachers, managers, maybe people in institutions, that sort of thing. And I can’t point to some earlier situation where someone really tricked me or betrayed me in a way that stayed with me. Nothing like, “Oh, that’s where this comes from.” I’ve had frustrations, and times where I felt people weren’t being fully straightforward, but not some major past event that stands out.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not in any significant way that I’m aware of. If you mean something smaller or more everyday, maybe, but nothing I’d identify as a big formative thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your coworkers or management would agree with your perception of being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, I don’t think they would agree with me — at least not out loud, and not in the way I’m saying it.\n\nManagement definitely doesn’t agree. They keep pointing to the logs and the policy documents and saying, “This is just the ticket rotation,” or “Everyone is subject to the same security audits,” or “There’s nothing unusual here.” But that’s part of what’s so frustrating, because it’s all presented as normal procedure. On paper it looks clean. That’s exactly the problem — it creates this paper trail where, later, they can say I missed things or mishandled tickets or triggered too many audit flags, and it all sounds objective.\n\nSome coworkers might say they haven’t noticed anything, or they might not want to get involved. I understand that. People have their own jobs to protect. But from my side, over the past year, the way the ticket rotation has changed and the way the audits seem to land, it just feels too consistent to be random. When I’ve tried to raise it, I get these explanations like it’s all policy, but the pattern is still there.\n\nSo if you asked them directly, no, they’d probably say I’m misreading it or overthinking it. But I don’t think that means it isn’t happening. It just means they have the documentation arranged in a way that makes it very hard to challenge.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anyone who could convince you that you're not being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose in theory, yes. I don’t want to say nothing could convince me, because I know how that sounds. If someone could actually show me, in a way that isn’t just management’s own logs and policy language, that the ticket rotation really is random and that the security audits are genuinely being applied the same way to everyone, then I would have to consider that.\n\nBut that’s the problem. Every time I ask, they show me the same kind of documentation — the ticket records, the audit notes, the procedure manual — and say, “See, this is just how it works.” But the whole concern I have is that those procedures are being used to create a paper trail against me. So just pointing back to the paper trail doesn’t really reassure me. It feels like asking the fox to audit the henhouse, if that makes sense.\n\nMaybe if there were some completely independent review, someone outside my management chain, someone who could look at the ticket assignments and the audit triggers over the past year and compare them fairly, not just repeat what my supervisor says — maybe that would matter. Or if I could see that other people were getting the same pattern of difficult tickets or the same level of audit attention, that would be different.\n\nBut so far, no one has shown me that. They just keep saying it’s policy. And I’m not trying to be stubborn, but after more than a year of seeing the same pattern, it’s hard for me to just accept “policy” as an explanation. So could I be convinced? Maybe. But it would have to be something genuinely independent and clear, not just management telling me again that I’m imagining a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or incident at work that first made you feel like you were being targeted?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a general point where it started to feel different. I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic incident, like one meeting or one email where I thought, “That’s it, they’re targeting me.” It was more that things changed around the time the hybrid schedule started, and then the after-hours ticket process changed too.\n\nThat was about a year and two months ago, roughly. Once those changes went into effect, that’s when I started noticing the pattern. At first I tried to explain it as just adjustment problems, you know, everyone getting used to the new schedule, new coverage expectations, new ticket flow. But over time it felt like the same kinds of things kept landing on me, especially with the after-hours tickets and how they were being tracked.\n\nSo if you’re asking when it began, I’d say it started around then — when the hybrid schedule and the after-hours ticket changes were introduced. Not one single incident, exactly, but that period was the beginning of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that management is targeting you could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… could it be wrong? I suppose I have to leave some room for that, because I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I know management keeps saying it’s just the new ticket rotation, that the security audits are routine, that the logs show everything is being handled according to policy. I understand that is what they’re saying.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, it doesn’t feel like a misunderstanding. For over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the way the tickets are being assigned and the way the audits are being used is building a paper trail against me. And every time I raise it, they point back to the same logs and policies that I think are part of the problem.\n\nSo… yes, technically, I guess I could be wrong. I’m not saying I’m incapable of being wrong. But I don’t feel like anyone has shown me a convincing reason to believe I’m wrong. They’ve just shown me documentation and said, “See, it’s policy.” And that doesn’t answer the concern, because the concern is that the policy and the documentation are being used in a way that makes it look normal while singling me out.\n\nSo I’d say I’m open to being proven wrong, but I don’t think I am wrong based on what I’ve seen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’ve thought about that, yes. I mean, I’m not going to pretend stress doesn’t affect how a person sees things. This has been going on for over a year, and it wears on you. When you’re constantly watching the ticket queue, checking which assignments come in after hours, looking at audit notices and wondering why your name keeps coming up, of course that makes you anxious. I’m aware of that.

But I don’t think stress explains the whole thing. That’s the part I keep coming back to. I’m here because I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but that doesn’t address what I’m actually concerned about. If the process itself is being used to make it look normal, then the logs are just part of the same problem.

So could stress make me more alert to it? Maybe. Could it make me dwell on it more than I otherwise would? Probably. But I don’t think this is just anxiety inventing something out of nowhere. From my perspective, the pattern is there, and I’ve been trying to get people to look at it seriously instead of dismissing it as me being stressed.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F60.0, F60.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… the main reason I’m here is because I feel like nobody at work is really listening to me about what’s been going on. I work in computer support, and for more than a year now, there’s been this new ticket rotation and these security audits, and I know management keeps saying it’s just “policy” and they keep showing me logs like that’s supposed to settle it, but it doesn’t.\n\nThe way it’s being handled, it feels very much like they’re building a paper trail against me. Like, every ticket assignment, every audit note, every little timing thing—there’s a record, and then later it’s used in a way that makes me look careless or problematic. They’ll say, “No, this is standard procedure,” but I’ve been in this field long enough to know when procedures are being applied in a particular direction.\n\nI’m not here because I just dislike change or don’t understand how audits work. I do. I’ve worked with systems and logs for years. That’s actually part of why I can see the pattern. I’ve tried to explain it, but it gets brushed off. So I guess I’m here because I need someone to take seriously that this isn’t just random workplace policy. It feels like it’s aimed at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like people are out to get you or watching you outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? No, that’s not really what I’m talking about. I’m not saying strangers are following me around or anything like that. The thing I’m trying to get across is specifically at work — the ticket rotation, the security audits, the way management keeps pointing to logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” as if that explains everything.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, over more than a year, it looks like those policies are being used in a very particular way to build a paper trail against me. Every little assignment, every audit note, it gets recorded, and then later it can be made to look like I’m the problem. That’s what I need someone to take seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management is intentionally trying to make you look bad?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t see how else to interpret it at this point. It’s been over a year of this new ticket rotation and the security audits, and every time I try to point out how it’s affecting me, management just pulls up logs and says, “See, it’s policy,” like that settles the whole matter.\n\nBut logs can be used selectively. Policies can be applied in ways that look neutral on paper but still create a record that makes one person look careless or difficult. That’s what I’m saying. I work in computer support, I understand ticket systems and audit trails. I’m not confused about how records work. The issue is that the pattern keeps landing on me in a way that builds this paper trail.\n\nSo yes, I do think they’re intentionally setting things up so that later they can say, “Look at all these incidents,” or “Look at these audit notes,” and make me look like I’m the problem. That’s why I’m here — because I’ve been trying to get someone to take that seriously instead of just accepting their explanation that it’s all routine policy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any other explanation for why management might be acting this way towards you, or is it definitely a deliberate attempt to make you look bad?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to consider other explanations, honestly. I mean, I’m not saying I jumped to this overnight. If it had been one or two ticket assignments, or one audit note, I could say, fine, maybe that’s just how the system works, maybe it’s just a new process being awkwardly implemented.\n\nBut it’s been over a year now. The new ticket rotation, the security audits, the way they keep pointing to the logs like the logs are some neutral final answer — that’s the part that bothers me. Because logs don’t explain intent. They just show what was recorded. And what gets recorded, and how it gets interpreted later, can absolutely be shaped.\n\nSo, is there theoretically another explanation? I suppose someone could say it’s just policy, or bad management, or some clumsy rollout. But from what I’ve seen, it doesn’t feel random or clumsy. It feels directed. It feels like the system is being used to build a paper trail against me, so later they can make me look like the problem. That’s why I’m here — because I need someone to understand that I’m not just complaining about paperwork. I’m saying the paperwork is the mechanism.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're being singled out or treated unfairly in situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of work. I mean, I don’t walk around thinking everybody is treating me unfairly or singling me out in general. That’s not what I’m trying to say.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is specifically at work, and specifically the way this has been going on for over a year with the ticket rotation and the security audits. That’s where I feel singled out. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t answer what I’m actually raising. A log only shows what got recorded. It doesn’t show why things are being assigned the way they are, or how those records might be used later.\n\nSo outside of work, no, I can’t say I notice that. But at work, yes, absolutely. It feels like the rotation and the audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, and I’ve been trying to get someone to take that seriously instead of just accepting management’s explanation that it’s all routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way about being singled out at work consistently over the past year, or have there been times when you didn't feel that way?\n\nPatient: It’s been consistent since the hybrid schedule changed and they changed the after-hours ticket process. That was about… a year and two months ago now, give or take.\n\nBefore that, I wouldn’t have described it this way. Work had normal frustrations, sure, but I didn’t feel like there was this ongoing pattern aimed at me. Once the hybrid schedule started and the after-hours tickets were handled differently, that’s when I began noticing how things kept landing in a way that made me look bad on paper.\n\nThere may have been individual days where I was too busy to think about it, or I tried to tell myself, “Maybe this is just the new system settling in.” But overall, no, it hasn’t really gone away. For about fourteen months it’s felt like the same thing repeating — assignments, audits, records — all of it creating this trail that points back at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything you could do, or any change that could happen at work, that would make you stop feeling like they're building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. If there were a real change, not just them saying, “Here are the logs, it’s policy,” then maybe. That’s the problem — they keep acting like showing me a log should end the discussion. But the log is part of what I’m concerned about. It’s not reassuring to me when the thing I think is being used to build the case is also what they hold up as proof that nothing is happening.\n\nIf they stopped using the new ticket rotation in a way that keeps making me look responsible for things after the fact, and if the security audits weren’t constantly being interpreted in the worst possible light, then maybe I could start to feel differently. But it would have to be transparent. I’d need to see that the assignments are actually fair, that the audits are being applied the same way to everyone, and that there isn’t some file being built where every little thing gets stacked up against me.\n\nI’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the ticket rotation and those audits are being used as a paper trail against me at work. Management keeps telling me it’s just policy, and they keep pointing to logs, but that doesn’t answer the real concern. So I suppose if someone independent looked at it — really looked at the pattern, not just the surface records — and if the process changed so it couldn’t be used that way anymore, then maybe I could stop feeling that way.\n\nBut if nothing changes and they just keep saying, “The logs show this,” then no, I don’t think I can just decide to stop seeing what’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found any concrete evidence to support your belief that management is intentionally building a case against you?\n\nPatient: Concrete evidence… that’s where this gets frustrating, because management keeps trying to define evidence only as what’s in their logs. They’ll show me the ticket records or the audit notes and say, basically, “See, this is just the normal process.” But to me, that doesn’t settle it. That’s part of the issue.\n\nI don’t have, you know, an email where someone says, “Let’s build a case against him.” If that’s what you mean by concrete evidence, no, I haven’t found something like that. But I also don’t think people would be careless enough to put it that plainly.\n\nWhat I have is the pattern. For over a year now, the new ticket rotation and the security audits keep producing this paper trail where I end up looking responsible or careless, and then when I question it, they point back to the same records and say it’s policy. It feels circular. The records are what I’m worried are being used against me, and then the records are also what they use to tell me I’m wrong.\n\nSo no, not a smoking gun, I guess. But I don’t think that means there’s nothing happening. It just means it’s being done through the system — through assignments, audits, documentation — in a way that looks routine from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences in the past where you felt like you were targeted or victimized?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in the same way, no. I’ve had normal workplace disagreements before, or times where I thought a manager didn’t like me, but that’s different from this.\n\nThis is the first time it has felt organized through the actual system — the ticket rotation, the audit notes, the documentation. That’s why I keep coming back to it, because it isn’t just someone making a rude comment or giving me a bad assignment one day. It’s that for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nAnd management keeps saying, “No, look, here are the logs, it’s just policy,” but that’s exactly what bothers me. The logs are the thing that make it look official. So if you’re asking whether I’ve always gone around feeling targeted, no, I wouldn’t say that. This feels specific to what’s been happening at work, and it started with those process changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're being targeted or treated unfairly in any other areas of your life besides work?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn’t say I feel targeted everywhere or by random people or anything like that. It’s not like I think the grocery store clerk is watching me or my neighbors are involved. It’s very specifically work.\n\nThat’s part of why it bothers me so much, honestly, because I know how it sounds when I keep coming back to it, but it isn’t some general feeling that the world is against me. It’s tied to this one situation — the ticket rotation, the security audits, the way the documentation keeps lining up so that I’m the one who looks responsible after the fact. And then management keeps saying, “Here are the logs, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t actually reassure me, because the logs are the thing I’m worried they’re using to build the paper trail.\n\nSo outside of work, no, I don’t really have that same feeling. My concern is that at work, for over a year now, the system has been used in a way that makes it look official and neutral, when to me the pattern looks like they’re building a case against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past that might be contributing to your feelings of being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve thought about that, because I know people ask that kind of question for a reason, and I’m not trying to dismiss it. But I can’t point to some traumatic thing in my past that would explain this.\n\nI’ve had stressful periods, sure — work stress, family stress, the kind of things most people have by the time they’re my age — but nothing where I’d say, “Yes, that’s why I feel this way at work now.” This feels tied to what has actually been happening there, with the ticket rotation and the audits and the documentation. It doesn’t feel like an old wound getting stirred up, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, not that I’m aware of. I don’t have some past event that I think is driving it. My concern is really about the pattern at work now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been bullied or harassed at any point in your life, either in a workplace or elsewhere?\n\nPatient: Not in any major way, no. I mean, I’m sure there were times growing up where someone was unkind, or jobs where people were difficult, but I wouldn’t call it bullying or harassment in the way people usually mean that.\n\nI’ve had coworkers I didn’t get along with, managers who were impatient or dismissive, things like that. But nothing where I felt like I was being systematically bullied for years or anything outside of normal workplace conflict. I don’t have some long history of being singled out, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nThis situation at work now feels different to me, but in terms of earlier in my life, no — nothing really stands out as bullying or harassment that I’d point to as a big factor.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a situation in the past where you felt misled or deceived by someone in a position of authority?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of a clear example of that, no. I mean, I’ve had bosses or supervisors make decisions I didn’t agree with, or say one thing and then later the policy seemed to change, but I don’t know that I’d call that being deceived exactly.\n\nWhen you say “position of authority,” I’m thinking teachers, managers, maybe people in institutions, that sort of thing. And I can’t point to some earlier situation where someone really tricked me or betrayed me in a way that stayed with me. Nothing like, “Oh, that’s where this comes from.” I’ve had frustrations, and times where I felt people weren’t being fully straightforward, but not some major past event that stands out.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not in any significant way that I’m aware of. If you mean something smaller or more everyday, maybe, but nothing I’d identify as a big formative thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your coworkers or management would agree with your perception of being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, I don’t think they would agree with me — at least not out loud, and not in the way I’m saying it.\n\nManagement definitely doesn’t agree. They keep pointing to the logs and the policy documents and saying, “This is just the ticket rotation,” or “Everyone is subject to the same security audits,” or “There’s nothing unusual here.” But that’s part of what’s so frustrating, because it’s all presented as normal procedure. On paper it looks clean. That’s exactly the problem — it creates this paper trail where, later, they can say I missed things or mishandled tickets or triggered too many audit flags, and it all sounds objective.\n\nSome coworkers might say they haven’t noticed anything, or they might not want to get involved. I understand that. People have their own jobs to protect. But from my side, over the past year, the way the ticket rotation has changed and the way the audits seem to land, it just feels too consistent to be random. When I’ve tried to raise it, I get these explanations like it’s all policy, but the pattern is still there.\n\nSo if you asked them directly, no, they’d probably say I’m misreading it or overthinking it. But I don’t think that means it isn’t happening. It just means they have the documentation arranged in a way that makes it very hard to challenge.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anyone who could convince you that you're not being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose in theory, yes. I don’t want to say nothing could convince me, because I know how that sounds. If someone could actually show me, in a way that isn’t just management’s own logs and policy language, that the ticket rotation really is random and that the security audits are genuinely being applied the same way to everyone, then I would have to consider that.\n\nBut that’s the problem. Every time I ask, they show me the same kind of documentation — the ticket records, the audit notes, the procedure manual — and say, “See, this is just how it works.” But the whole concern I have is that those procedures are being used to create a paper trail against me. So just pointing back to the paper trail doesn’t really reassure me. It feels like asking the fox to audit the henhouse, if that makes sense.\n\nMaybe if there were some completely independent review, someone outside my management chain, someone who could look at the ticket assignments and the audit triggers over the past year and compare them fairly, not just repeat what my supervisor says — maybe that would matter. Or if I could see that other people were getting the same pattern of difficult tickets or the same level of audit attention, that would be different.\n\nBut so far, no one has shown me that. They just keep saying it’s policy. And I’m not trying to be stubborn, but after more than a year of seeing the same pattern, it’s hard for me to just accept “policy” as an explanation. So could I be convinced? Maybe. But it would have to be something genuinely independent and clear, not just management telling me again that I’m imagining a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or incident at work that first made you feel like you were being targeted?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a general point where it started to feel different. I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic incident, like one meeting or one email where I thought, “That’s it, they’re targeting me.” It was more that things changed around the time the hybrid schedule started, and then the after-hours ticket process changed too.\n\nThat was about a year and two months ago, roughly. Once those changes went into effect, that’s when I started noticing the pattern. At first I tried to explain it as just adjustment problems, you know, everyone getting used to the new schedule, new coverage expectations, new ticket flow. But over time it felt like the same kinds of things kept landing on me, especially with the after-hours tickets and how they were being tracked.\n\nSo if you’re asking when it began, I’d say it started around then — when the hybrid schedule and the after-hours ticket changes were introduced. Not one single incident, exactly, but that period was the beginning of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that management is targeting you could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… could it be wrong? I suppose I have to leave some room for that, because I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I know management keeps saying it’s just the new ticket rotation, that the security audits are routine, that the logs show everything is being handled according to policy. I understand that is what they’re saying.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, it doesn’t feel like a misunderstanding. For over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the way the tickets are being assigned and the way the audits are being used is building a paper trail against me. And every time I raise it, they point back to the same logs and policies that I think are part of the problem.\n\nSo… yes, technically, I guess I could be wrong. I’m not saying I’m incapable of being wrong. But I don’t feel like anyone has shown me a convincing reason to believe I’m wrong. They’ve just shown me documentation and said, “See, it’s policy.” And that doesn’t answer the concern, because the concern is that the policy and the documentation are being used in a way that makes it look normal while singling me out.\n\nSo I’d say I’m open to being proven wrong, but I don’t think I am wrong based on what I’ve seen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of being targeted at work could be influenced by your own stress or anxiety rather than an actual plot against you?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yes. I mean, I’m not going to pretend stress doesn’t affect how a person sees things. This has been going on for over a year, and it wears on you. When you’re constantly watching the ticket queue, checking which assignments come in after hours, looking at audit notices and wondering why your name keeps coming up, of course that makes you anxious. I’m aware of that.\n\nBut I don’t think stress explains the whole thing. That’s the part I keep coming back to. I’m here because I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but that doesn’t address what I’m actually concerned about. If the process itself is being used to make it look normal, then the logs are just part of the same problem.\n\nSo could stress make me more alert to it? Maybe. Could it make me dwell on it more than I otherwise would? Probably. But I don’t think this is just anxiety inventing something out of nowhere. I don’t have some long history of being singled out, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nThis situation at work now feels different to me, but in terms of earlier in my life, no — nothing really stands out as bullying or harassment that I’d point to as a big factor.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a situation in the past where you felt misled or deceived by someone in a position of authority?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of a clear example of that, no. I mean, I’ve had bosses or supervisors make decisions I didn’t agree with, or say one thing and then later the policy seemed to change, but I don’t know that I’d call that being deceived exactly.\n\nWhen you say “position of authority,” I’m thinking teachers, managers, maybe people in institutions, that sort of thing. And I can’t point to some earlier situation where someone really tricked me or betrayed me in a way that stayed with me. Nothing like, “Oh, that’s where this comes from.” I’ve had frustrations, and times where I felt people weren’t being fully straightforward, but not some major past event that stands out.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not in any significant way that I’m aware of. If you mean something smaller or more everyday, maybe, but nothing I’d identify as a big formative thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your coworkers or management would agree with your perception of being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, I don’t think they would agree with me — at least not out loud, and not in the way I’m saying it.\n\nManagement definitely doesn’t agree. They keep pointing to the logs and the policy documents and saying, “This is just the ticket rotation,” or “Everyone is subject to the same security audits,” or “There’s nothing unusual here.” But that’s part of what’s so frustrating, because it’s all presented as normal procedure. On paper it looks clean. That’s exactly the problem — it creates this paper trail where, later, they can say I missed things or mishandled tickets or triggered too many audit flags, and it all sounds objective.\n\nSome coworkers might say they haven’t noticed anything, or they might not want to get involved. I understand that. People have their own jobs to protect. But from my side, over the past year, the way the ticket rotation has changed and the way the audits seem to land, it just feels too consistent to be random. When I’ve tried to raise it, I get these explanations like it’s all policy, but the pattern is still there.\n\nSo if you asked them directly, no, they’d probably say I’m misreading it or overthinking it. But I don’t think that means it isn’t happening. It just means they have the documentation arranged in a way that makes it very hard to challenge.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anyone who could convince you that you're not being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose in theory, yes. I don’t want to say nothing could convince me, because I know how that sounds. If someone could actually show me, in a way that isn’t just management’s own logs and policy language, that the ticket rotation really is random and that the security audits are genuinely being applied the same way to everyone, then I would have to consider that.\n\nBut that’s the problem. Every time I ask, they show me the same kind of documentation — the ticket records, the audit notes, the procedure manual — and say, “See, this is just how it works.” But the whole concern I have is that those procedures are being used to create a paper trail against me. So just pointing back to the paper trail doesn’t really reassure me. It feels like asking the fox to audit the henhouse, if that makes sense.\n\nMaybe if there were some completely independent review, someone outside my management chain, someone who could look at the ticket assignments and the audit triggers over the past year and compare them fairly, not just repeat what my supervisor says — maybe that would matter. Or if I could see that other people were getting the same pattern of difficult tickets or the same level of audit attention, that would be different.\n\nBut so far, no one has shown me that. They just keep saying it’s policy. And I’m not trying to be stubborn, but after more than a year of seeing the same pattern, it’s hard for me to just accept “policy” as an explanation. So could I be convinced? Maybe. But it would have to be something genuinely independent and clear, not just management telling me again that I’m imagining a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or incident at work that first made you feel like you were being targeted?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a general point where it started to feel different. I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic incident, like one meeting or one email where I thought, “That’s it, they’re targeting me.” It was more that things changed around the time the hybrid schedule started, and then the after-hours ticket process changed too.\n\nThat was about a year and two months ago, roughly. Once those changes went into effect, that’s when I started noticing the pattern. At first I tried to explain it as just adjustment problems, you know, everyone getting used to the new schedule, new coverage expectations, new ticket flow. But over time it felt like the same kinds of things kept landing on me, especially with the after-hours tickets and how they were being tracked.\n\nSo if you’re asking when it began, I’d say it started around then — when the hybrid schedule and the after-hours ticket changes were introduced. Not one single incident, exactly, but that period was the beginning of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that management is targeting you could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… could it be wrong? I suppose I have to leave some room for that, because I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I know management keeps saying it’s just the new ticket rotation, that the security audits are routine, that the logs show everything is being handled according to policy. I understand that is what they’re saying.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, it doesn’t feel like a misunderstanding. For over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the way the tickets are being assigned and the way the audits are being used is building a paper trail against me. And every time I raise it, they point back to the same logs and policies that I think are part of the problem.\n\nSo… yes, technically, I guess I could be wrong. I’m not saying I’m incapable of being wrong. But I don’t feel like anyone has shown me a convincing reason to believe I’m wrong. They’ve just shown me documentation and said, “See, it’s policy.” And that doesn’t answer the concern, because the concern is that the policy and the documentation are being used in a way that makes it look normal while singling me out.\n\nSo I’d say I’m open to being proven wrong, but I don’t think I am wrong based on what I’ve seen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of being targeted at work could be influenced by your own stress or anxiety rather than an actual plot against you?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yes. I mean, I’m not going to pretend stress doesn’t affect how a person sees things. This has been going on for over a year, and it wears on you. When you’re constantly watching the ticket queue, checking which assignments come in after hours, looking at audit notices and wondering why your name keeps coming up, of course that makes you anxious. I’m aware of that.\n\nBut I don’t think stress explains the whole thing. That’s the part I keep coming back to. I’m here because I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but that doesn’t address what I’m actually concerned about. If the process itself is being used to make it look normal, then the logs are just part of the same problem.\n\nSo could stress make me more alert to it? Maybe. Could it make me dwell on it more than I otherwise would? Probably. But I don’t think this is just anxiety inventing something out of nowhere. From my perspective, the pattern is there, and I’ve been trying to get people to look at it seriously instead of dismissing it as me being stressed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say I’m completely certain in the sense that nothing could ever change my mind. I don’t want to sound like I’m saying I’m beyond reason or that no evidence would matter. If someone could actually show me, clearly, that the ticket rotation and the security audits were being applied evenly and that there wasn’t this pattern of things being documented around me, then yes, I’d have to look at that.

But the problem is, that’s what management says they’re already showing me. They show me logs, they point to policy, they say, “This is just how the system works.” And I understand that those are supposed to be the facts. I’m not ignoring that. It’s just that, from my side, those same logs and policies are exactly what make it hard to trust the explanation, because my concern is that the process is being used to build a paper trail against me while making it look routine.

So could I change my mind with different information? In theory, yes. If it was really independent, if it actually addressed the pattern I’m talking about instead of just repeating “it’s policy,” I’d listen. But as things stand, no, I don’t feel like my mind has been changed. I’ve been trying for over a year to get someone to take this seriously, and so far the explanations I’ve been given don’t explain away what I’m seeing.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F60.0, F60.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… the main reason I’m here is because I feel like nobody at work is really listening to me about what’s been going on. I work in computer support, and for more than a year now, there’s been this new ticket rotation and these security audits, and I know management keeps saying it’s just “policy” and they keep showing me logs like that’s supposed to settle it, but it doesn’t.\n\nThe way it’s being handled, it feels very much like they’re building a paper trail against me. Like, every ticket assignment, every audit note, every little timing thing—there’s a record, and then later it’s used in a way that makes me look careless or problematic. They’ll say, “No, this is standard procedure,” but I’ve been in this field long enough to know when procedures are being applied in a particular direction.\n\nI’m not here because I just dislike change or don’t understand how audits work. I do. I’ve worked with systems and logs for years. That’s actually part of why I can see the pattern. I’ve tried to explain it, but it gets brushed off. So I guess I’m here because I need someone to take seriously that this isn’t just random workplace policy. It feels like it’s aimed at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like people are out to get you or watching you outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? No, that’s not really what I’m talking about. I’m not saying strangers are following me around or anything like that. The thing I’m trying to get across is specifically at work — the ticket rotation, the security audits, the way management keeps pointing to logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” as if that explains everything.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, over more than a year, it looks like those policies are being used in a very particular way to build a paper trail against me. Every little assignment, every audit note, it gets recorded, and then later it can be made to look like I’m the problem. That’s what I need someone to take seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management is intentionally trying to make you look bad?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t see how else to interpret it at this point. It’s been over a year of this new ticket rotation and the security audits, and every time I try to point out how it’s affecting me, management just pulls up logs and says, “See, it’s policy,” like that settles the whole matter.\n\nBut logs can be used selectively. Policies can be applied in ways that look neutral on paper but still create a record that makes one person look careless or difficult. That’s what I’m saying. I work in computer support, I understand ticket systems and audit trails. I’m not confused about how records work. The issue is that the pattern keeps landing on me in a way that builds this paper trail.\n\nSo yes, I do think they’re intentionally setting things up so that later they can say, “Look at all these incidents,” or “Look at these audit notes,” and make me look like I’m the problem. That’s why I’m here — because I’ve been trying to get someone to take that seriously instead of just accepting their explanation that it’s all routine policy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any other explanation for why management might be acting this way towards you, or is it definitely a deliberate attempt to make you look bad?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to consider other explanations, honestly. I mean, I’m not saying I jumped to this overnight. If it had been one or two ticket assignments, or one audit note, I could say, fine, maybe that’s just how the system works, maybe it’s just a new process being awkwardly implemented.\n\nBut it’s been over a year now. The new ticket rotation, the security audits, the way they keep pointing to the logs like the logs are some neutral final answer — that’s the part that bothers me. Because logs don’t explain intent. They just show what was recorded. And what gets recorded, and how it gets interpreted later, can absolutely be shaped.\n\nSo, is there theoretically another explanation? I suppose someone could say it’s just policy, or bad management, or some clumsy rollout. But from what I’ve seen, it doesn’t feel random or clumsy. It feels directed. It feels like the system is being used to build a paper trail against me, so later they can make me look like the problem. That’s why I’m here — because I need someone to understand that I’m not just complaining about paperwork. I’m saying the paperwork is the mechanism.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're being singled out or treated unfairly in situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of work. I mean, I don’t walk around thinking everybody is treating me unfairly or singling me out in general. That’s not what I’m trying to say.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is specifically at work, and specifically the way this has been going on for over a year with the ticket rotation and the security audits. That’s where I feel singled out. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t answer what I’m actually raising. A log only shows what got recorded. It doesn’t show why things are being assigned the way they are, or how those records might be used later.\n\nSo outside of work, no, I can’t say I notice that. But at work, yes, absolutely. It feels like the rotation and the audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, and I’ve been trying to get someone to take that seriously instead of just accepting management’s explanation that it’s all routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way about being singled out at work consistently over the past year, or have there been times when you didn't feel that way?\n\nPatient: It’s been consistent since the hybrid schedule changed and they changed the after-hours ticket process. That was about… a year and two months ago now, give or take.\n\nBefore that, I wouldn’t have described it this way. Work had normal frustrations, sure, but I didn’t feel like there was this ongoing pattern aimed at me. Once the hybrid schedule started and the after-hours tickets were handled differently, that’s when I began noticing how things kept landing in a way that made me look bad on paper.\n\nThere may have been individual days where I was too busy to think about it, or I tried to tell myself, “Maybe this is just the new system settling in.” But overall, no, it hasn’t really gone away. For about fourteen months it’s felt like the same thing repeating — assignments, audits, records — all of it creating this trail that points back at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything you could do, or any change that could happen at work, that would make you stop feeling like they're building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. If there were a real change, not just them saying, “Here are the logs, it’s policy,” then maybe. That’s the problem — they keep acting like showing me a log should end the discussion. But the log is part of what I’m concerned about. It’s not reassuring to me when the thing I think is being used to build the case is also what they hold up as proof that nothing is happening.\n\nIf they stopped using the new ticket rotation in a way that keeps making me look responsible for things after the fact, and if the security audits weren’t constantly being interpreted in the worst possible light, then maybe I could start to feel differently. But it would have to be transparent. I’d need to see that the assignments are actually fair, that the audits are being applied the same way to everyone, and that there isn’t some file being built where every little thing gets stacked up against me.\n\nI’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the ticket rotation and those audits are being used as a paper trail against me at work. Management keeps telling me it’s just policy, and they keep pointing to logs, but that doesn’t answer the real concern. So I suppose if someone independent looked at it — really looked at the pattern, not just the surface records — and if the process changed so it couldn’t be used that way anymore, then maybe I could stop feeling that way.\n\nBut if nothing changes and they just keep saying, “The logs show this,” then no, I don’t think I can just decide to stop seeing what’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found any concrete evidence to support your belief that management is intentionally building a case against you?\n\nPatient: Concrete evidence… that’s where this gets frustrating, because management keeps trying to define evidence only as what’s in their logs. They’ll show me the ticket records or the audit notes and say, basically, “See, this is just the normal process.” But to me, that doesn’t settle it. That’s part of the issue.\n\nI don’t have, you know, an email where someone says, “Let’s build a case against him.” If that’s what you mean by concrete evidence, no, I haven’t found something like that. But I also don’t think people would be careless enough to put it that plainly.\n\nWhat I have is the pattern. For over a year now, the new ticket rotation and the security audits keep producing this paper trail where I end up looking responsible or careless, and then when I question it, they point back to the same records and say it’s policy. It feels circular. The records are what I’m worried are being used against me, and then the records are also what they use to tell me I’m wrong.\n\nSo no, not a smoking gun, I guess. But I don’t think that means there’s nothing happening. It just means it’s being done through the system — through assignments, audits, documentation — in a way that looks routine from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences in the past where you felt like you were targeted or victimized?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in the same way, no. I’ve had normal workplace disagreements before, or times where I thought a manager didn’t like me, but that’s different from this.\n\nThis is the first time it has felt organized through the actual system — the ticket rotation, the audit notes, the documentation. That’s why I keep coming back to it, because it isn’t just someone making a rude comment or giving me a bad assignment one day. It’s that for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nAnd management keeps saying, “No, look, here are the logs, it’s just policy,” but that’s exactly what bothers me. The logs are the thing that make it look official. So if you’re asking whether I’ve always gone around feeling targeted, no, I wouldn’t say that. This feels specific to what’s been happening at work, and it started with those process changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're being targeted or treated unfairly in any other areas of your life besides work?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn’t say I feel targeted everywhere or by random people or anything like that. It’s not like I think the grocery store clerk is watching me or my neighbors are involved. It’s very specifically work.\n\nThat’s part of why it bothers me so much, honestly, because I know how it sounds when I keep coming back to it, but it isn’t some general feeling that the world is against me. It’s tied to this one situation — the ticket rotation, the security audits, the way the documentation keeps lining up so that I’m the one who looks responsible after the fact. And then management keeps saying, “Here are the logs, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t actually reassure me, because the logs are the thing I’m worried they’re using to build the paper trail.\n\nSo outside of work, no, I don’t really have that same feeling. My concern is that at work, for over a year now, the system has been used in a way that makes it look official and neutral, when to me the pattern looks like they’re building a case against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past that might be contributing to your feelings of being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve thought about that, because I know people ask that kind of question for a reason, and I’m not trying to dismiss it. But I can’t point to some traumatic thing in my past that would explain this.\n\nI’ve had stressful periods, sure — work stress, family stress, the kind of things most people have by the time they’re my age — but nothing where I’d say, “Yes, that’s why I feel this way at work now.” This feels tied to what has actually been happening there, with the ticket rotation and the audits and the documentation. It doesn’t feel like an old wound getting stirred up, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, not that I’m aware of. I don’t have some past event that I think is driving it. My concern is really about the pattern at work now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been bullied or harassed at any point in your life, either in a workplace or elsewhere?\n\nPatient: Not in any major way, no. I mean, I’m sure there were times growing up where someone was unkind, or jobs where people were difficult, but I wouldn’t call it bullying or harassment in the way people usually mean that.\n\nI’ve had coworkers I didn’t get along with, managers who were impatient or dismissive, things like that. But nothing where I felt like I was being systematically bullied for years or anything outside of normal workplace conflict. I don’t have some long history of being singled out, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nThis situation at work now feels different to me, but in terms of earlier in my life, no — nothing really stands out as bullying or harassment that I’d point to as a big factor.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a situation in the past where you felt misled or deceived by someone in a position of authority?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of a clear example of that, no. I mean, I’ve had bosses or supervisors make decisions I didn’t agree with, or say one thing and then later the policy seemed to change, but I don’t know that I’d call that being deceived exactly.\n\nWhen you say “position of authority,” I’m thinking teachers, managers, maybe people in institutions, that sort of thing. And I can’t point to some earlier situation where someone really tricked me or betrayed me in a way that stayed with me. Nothing like, “Oh, that’s where this comes from.” I’ve had frustrations, and times where I felt people weren’t being fully straightforward, but not some major past event that stands out.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not in any significant way that I’m aware of. If you mean something smaller or more everyday, maybe, but nothing I’d identify as a big formative thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your coworkers or management would agree with your perception of being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, I don’t think they would agree with me — at least not out loud, and not in the way I’m saying it.\n\nManagement definitely doesn’t agree. They keep pointing to the logs and the policy documents and saying, “This is just the ticket rotation,” or “Everyone is subject to the same security audits,” or “There’s nothing unusual here.” But that’s part of what’s so frustrating, because it’s all presented as normal procedure. On paper it looks clean. That’s exactly the problem — it creates this paper trail where, later, they can say I missed things or mishandled tickets or triggered too many audit flags, and it all sounds objective.\n\nSome coworkers might say they haven’t noticed anything, or they might not want to get involved. I understand that. People have their own jobs to protect. But from my side, over the past year, the way the ticket rotation has changed and the way the audits seem to land, it just feels too consistent to be random. When I’ve tried to raise it, I get these explanations like it’s all policy, but the pattern is still there.\n\nSo if you asked them directly, no, they’d probably say I’m misreading it or overthinking it. But I don’t think that means it isn’t happening. It just means they have the documentation arranged in a way that makes it very hard to challenge.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anyone who could convince you that you're not being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose in theory, yes. I don’t want to say nothing could convince me, because I know how that sounds. If someone could actually show me, in a way that isn’t just management’s own logs and policy language, that the ticket rotation really is random and that the security audits are genuinely being applied the same way to everyone, then I would have to consider that.\n\nBut that’s the problem. Every time I ask, they show me the same kind of documentation — the ticket records, the audit notes, the procedure manual — and say, “See, this is just how it works.” But the whole concern I have is that those procedures are being used to create a paper trail against me. So just pointing back to the paper trail doesn’t really reassure me. It feels like asking the fox to audit the henhouse, if that makes sense.\n\nMaybe if there were some completely independent review, someone outside my management chain, someone who could look at the ticket assignments and the audit triggers over the past year and compare them fairly, not just repeat what my supervisor says — maybe that would matter. Or if I could see that other people were getting the same pattern of difficult tickets or the same level of audit attention, that would be different.\n\nBut so far, no one has shown me that. They just keep saying it’s policy. And I’m not trying to be stubborn, but after more than a year of seeing the same pattern, it’s hard for me to just accept “policy” as an explanation. So could I be convinced? Maybe. But it would have to be something genuinely independent and clear, not just management telling me again that I’m imagining a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or incident at work that first made you feel like you were being targeted?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a general point where it started to feel different. I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic incident, like one meeting or one email where I thought, “That’s it, they’re targeting me.” It was more that things changed around the time the hybrid schedule started, and then the after-hours ticket process changed too.\n\nThat was about a year and two months ago, roughly. Once those changes went into effect, that’s when I started noticing the pattern. At first I tried to explain it as just adjustment problems, you know, everyone getting used to the new schedule, new coverage expectations, new ticket flow. But over time it felt like the same kinds of things kept landing on me, especially with the after-hours tickets and how they were being tracked.\n\nSo if you’re asking when it began, I’d say it started around then — when the hybrid schedule and the after-hours ticket changes were introduced. Not one single incident, exactly, but that period was the beginning of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that management is targeting you could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… could it be wrong? I suppose I have to leave some room for that, because I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I know management keeps saying it’s just the new ticket rotation, that the security audits are routine, that the logs show everything is being handled according to policy. I understand that is what they’re saying.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, it doesn’t feel like a misunderstanding. For over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the way the tickets are being assigned and the way the audits are being used is building a paper trail against me. And every time I raise it, they point back to the same logs and policies that I think are part of the problem.\n\nSo… yes, technically, I guess I could be wrong. I’m not saying I’m incapable of being wrong. But I don’t feel like anyone has shown me a convincing reason to believe I’m wrong. They’ve just shown me documentation and said, “See, it’s policy.” And that doesn’t answer the concern, because the concern is that the policy and the documentation are being used in a way that makes it look normal while singling me out.\n\nSo I’d say I’m open to being proven wrong, but I don’t think I am wrong based on what I’ve seen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of being targeted at work could be influenced by your own stress or anxiety rather than an actual plot against you?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yes. I mean, I’m not going to pretend stress doesn’t affect how a person sees things. This has been going on for over a year, and it wears on you. When you’re constantly watching the ticket queue, checking which assignments come in after hours, looking at audit notices and wondering why your name keeps coming up, of course that makes you anxious. I’m aware of that.\n\nBut I don’t think stress explains the whole thing. That’s the part I keep coming back to. I’m here because I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but that doesn’t address what I’m actually concerned about. If the process itself is being used to make it look normal, then the logs are just part of the same problem.\n\nSo could stress make me more alert to it? Maybe. Could it make me dwell on it more than I otherwise would? Probably. But I don’t think this is just anxiety inventing something out of nowhere. From my perspective, the pattern is there, and I’ve been trying to get people to look at it seriously instead of dismissing it as me being stressed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your belief that management is targeting you is something you're completely certain about, or is it something you could potentially change your mind about if presented with different information?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m completely certain in the sense that nothing could ever change my mind. I don’t want to sound like I’m saying I’m beyond reason or that no evidence would matter. If someone could actually show me, clearly, that the ticket rotation and the security audits were being applied evenly and that there wasn’t this pattern of things being documented around me, then yes, I’d have to look at that.\n\nBut the problem is, that’s what management says they’re already showing me. They show me logs, they point to policy, they say, “This is just how the system works.” And I understand that those are supposed to be the facts. I’m not ignoring that. It’s just that, from my side, those same logs and policies are exactly what make it hard to trust the explanation, because my concern is that the process is being used to build a paper trail against me while making it look routine.\n\nSo could I change my mind with different information? In theory, yes. If it was really independent, if it actually addressed the pattern I’m talking about instead of just repeating “it’s policy,” I’d listen. I mean, I’ve had bosses or supervisors make decisions I didn’t agree with, or say one thing and then later the policy seemed to change, but I don’t know that I’d call that being deceived exactly.\n\nWhen you say “position of authority,” I’m thinking teachers, managers, maybe people in institutions, that sort of thing. And I can’t point to some earlier situation where someone really tricked me or betrayed me in a way that stayed with me. Nothing like, “Oh, that’s where this comes from.” I’ve had frustrations, and times where I felt people weren’t being fully straightforward, but not some major past event that stands out.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not in any significant way that I’m aware of. If you mean something smaller or more everyday, maybe, but nothing I’d identify as a big formative thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your coworkers or management would agree with your perception of being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, I don’t think they would agree with me — at least not out loud, and not in the way I’m saying it.\n\nManagement definitely doesn’t agree. They keep pointing to the logs and the policy documents and saying, “This is just the ticket rotation,” or “Everyone is subject to the same security audits,” or “There’s nothing unusual here.” But that’s part of what’s so frustrating, because it’s all presented as normal procedure. On paper it looks clean. That’s exactly the problem — it creates this paper trail where, later, they can say I missed things or mishandled tickets or triggered too many audit flags, and it all sounds objective.\n\nSome coworkers might say they haven’t noticed anything, or they might not want to get involved. I understand that. People have their own jobs to protect. But from my side, over the past year, the way the ticket rotation has changed and the way the audits seem to land, it just feels too consistent to be random. When I’ve tried to raise it, I get these explanations like it’s all policy, but the pattern is still there.\n\nSo if you asked them directly, no, they’d probably say I’m misreading it or overthinking it. But I don’t think that means it isn’t happening. It just means they have the documentation arranged in a way that makes it very hard to challenge.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anyone who could convince you that you're not being targeted at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose in theory, yes. I don’t want to say nothing could convince me, because I know how that sounds. If someone could actually show me, in a way that isn’t just management’s own logs and policy language, that the ticket rotation really is random and that the security audits are genuinely being applied the same way to everyone, then I would have to consider that.\n\nBut that’s the problem. Every time I ask, they show me the same kind of documentation — the ticket records, the audit notes, the procedure manual — and say, “See, this is just how it works.” But the whole concern I have is that those procedures are being used to create a paper trail against me. So just pointing back to the paper trail doesn’t really reassure me. It feels like asking the fox to audit the henhouse, if that makes sense.\n\nMaybe if there were some completely independent review, someone outside my management chain, someone who could look at the ticket assignments and the audit triggers over the past year and compare them fairly, not just repeat what my supervisor says — maybe that would matter. Or if I could see that other people were getting the same pattern of difficult tickets or the same level of audit attention, that would be different.\n\nBut so far, no one has shown me that. They just keep saying it’s policy. And I’m not trying to be stubborn, but after more than a year of seeing the same pattern, it’s hard for me to just accept “policy” as an explanation. So could I be convinced? Maybe. But it would have to be something genuinely independent and clear, not just management telling me again that I’m imagining a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or incident at work that first made you feel like you were being targeted?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a general point where it started to feel different. I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic incident, like one meeting or one email where I thought, “That’s it, they’re targeting me.” It was more that things changed around the time the hybrid schedule started, and then the after-hours ticket process changed too.\n\nThat was about a year and two months ago, roughly. Once those changes went into effect, that’s when I started noticing the pattern. At first I tried to explain it as just adjustment problems, you know, everyone getting used to the new schedule, new coverage expectations, new ticket flow. But over time it felt like the same kinds of things kept landing on me, especially with the after-hours tickets and how they were being tracked.\n\nSo if you’re asking when it began, I’d say it started around then — when the hybrid schedule and the after-hours ticket changes were introduced. Not one single incident, exactly, but that period was the beginning of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that management is targeting you could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… could it be wrong? I suppose I have to leave some room for that, because I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I know management keeps saying it’s just the new ticket rotation, that the security audits are routine, that the logs show everything is being handled according to policy. I understand that is what they’re saying.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, it doesn’t feel like a misunderstanding. For over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the way the tickets are being assigned and the way the audits are being used is building a paper trail against me. And every time I raise it, they point back to the same logs and policies that I think are part of the problem.\n\nSo… yes, technically, I guess I could be wrong. I’m not saying I’m incapable of being wrong. But I don’t feel like anyone has shown me a convincing reason to believe I’m wrong. They’ve just shown me documentation and said, “See, it’s policy.” And that doesn’t answer the concern, because the concern is that the policy and the documentation are being used in a way that makes it look normal while singling me out.\n\nSo I’d say I’m open to being proven wrong, but I don’t think I am wrong based on what I’ve seen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of being targeted at work could be influenced by your own stress or anxiety rather than an actual plot against you?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yes. I mean, I’m not going to pretend stress doesn’t affect how a person sees things. This has been going on for over a year, and it wears on you. When you’re constantly watching the ticket queue, checking which assignments come in after hours, looking at audit notices and wondering why your name keeps coming up, of course that makes you anxious. I’m aware of that.\n\nBut I don’t think stress explains the whole thing. That’s the part I keep coming back to. I’m here because I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but that doesn’t address what I’m actually concerned about. If the process itself is being used to make it look normal, then the logs are just part of the same problem.\n\nSo could stress make me more alert to it? Maybe. Could it make me dwell on it more than I otherwise would? Probably. But I don’t think this is just anxiety inventing something out of nowhere. From my perspective, the pattern is there, and I’ve been trying to get people to look at it seriously instead of dismissing it as me being stressed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your belief that management is targeting you is something you're completely certain about, or is it something you could potentially change your mind about if presented with different information?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m completely certain in the sense that nothing could ever change my mind. I don’t want to sound like I’m saying I’m beyond reason or that no evidence would matter. If someone could actually show me, clearly, that the ticket rotation and the security audits were being applied evenly and that there wasn’t this pattern of things being documented around me, then yes, I’d have to look at that.\n\nBut the problem is, that’s what management says they’re already showing me. They show me logs, they point to policy, they say, “This is just how the system works.” And I understand that those are supposed to be the facts. I’m not ignoring that. It’s just that, from my side, those same logs and policies are exactly what make it hard to trust the explanation, because my concern is that the process is being used to build a paper trail against me while making it look routine.\n\nSo could I change my mind with different information? In theory, yes. If it was really independent, if it actually addressed the pattern I’m talking about instead of just repeating “it’s policy,” I’d listen. But as things stand, no, I don’t feel like my mind has been changed. I wouldn’t say I distrust everyone, no. I don’t walk around thinking every person has some hidden agenda. I can work with people, talk to people, be normal with people. But at work, with management especially, yes, I do find myself questioning motives now, because of what’s been happening.

For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits aren’t just routine in how they’re being applied to me. It feels like they’re being used to build a paper trail against me. And when I bring that up, management keeps coming back with logs and saying, “See, it’s policy, it’s just the system.” But that doesn’t reassure me, because if the system is the method being used, then pointing to the system doesn’t really answer the concern.

So… generally, I’d say I’m cautious. Maybe more cautious than I used to be. I question motives when something doesn’t line up, especially when the same people who control the process are the ones telling me there’s nothing to see. That’s hard for me to just accept at face value.
